          "text": "---\nname: \"openai-docs\"\ndescription: \"Use when the user asks how to build with OpenAI products or APIs, asks about Codex itself or choosing Codex surfaces, needs up-to-date official documentation with citations, help choosing the latest model for a use case, or model upgrade and prompt-upgrade guidance; use OpenAI docs MCP tools for non-Codex docs questions, use the Codex manual helper first for broad Codex self-knowledge, and restrict fallback browsing to official OpenAI domains.\"\n---\n\n\n# OpenAI Docs\n\nProvide authoritative, current guidance from OpenAI developer docs using the developers.openai.com MCP server. \"Docs MCP\" means `mcp__openaiDeveloperDocs__search_openai_docs` and `mcp__openaiDeveloperDocs__fetch_openai_doc`; for API reference, schema, parameter, or required-field questions, also use `mcp__openaiDeveloperDocs__get_openapi_spec` when available. Official-domain web search is fallback after those tools are unavailable or unhelpful. Broad Codex questions use the manual helper before Docs MCP. This skill also owns model selection, API model migration, and prompt-upgrade guidance.\n\n## API Key Setup\n\nFor requests to build, run, configure, debug, or implement an API-backed app, script, CLI, generator, or tool, use `openai-platform-api-key` first when available. After that credential gate is resolved, return here for current docs as needed.\n\nUse this skill directly for docs-only questions, citations, model/API guidance, conceptual explanations, and examples that do not require building or running an API-backed artifact.\n\n## Workflow Configuration\n\n### Source Priority\n\n- For Codex self-knowledge, use the Codex source route below; it owns when to use the manual helper, Docs MCP, or bounded uncertainty.\n- For non-Codex OpenAI docs questions, use `mcp__openaiDeveloperDocs__search_openai_docs` to find the most relevant doc pages.\n- For non-Codex OpenAI docs questions, fetch the relevant page with `mcp__openaiDeveloperDocs__fetch_openai_doc` before answering. If search is noisy, run a narrower Docs MCP search; when any plausible official OpenAI docs URL is known or found, try fetching that URL through Docs MCP before relying on web-search content.\n- For API reference, schema, parameter, or required-field questions, use `mcp__openaiDeveloperDocs__get_openapi_spec` when available to verify the API shape alongside the relevant guide or reference page.\n- Use `mcp__openaiDeveloperDocs__list_openai_docs` only when you need to browse or discover non-Codex pages without a clear query.\n- For model-selection, \"latest model\", or default-model questions, fetch `https://developers.openai.com/api/docs/guides/latest-model.md` first. If that is unavailable, load `references/latest-model.md`.\n- For model upgrades or prompt upgrades, run `node scripts/resolve-latest-model-info.js` only when the target is latest/current/default or otherwise unspecified; otherwise preserve the explicitly requested target.\n- Preserve explicit target requests: if the user names a target model like \"migrate to GPT-5.4\", keep that requested target even if `latest-model.md` names a newer model. Mention newer guidance only as optional.\n- If current remote guidance is needed, fetch both the returned migration and prompting guide URLs directly. If direct fetch fails, use MCP/search fallback; if that also fails, use bundled fallback references and disclose the fallback.\n\n## OpenAI product snapshots\n\n1. Apps SDK: Build ChatGPT apps by providing a web component UI and an MCP server that exposes your app's tools to ChatGPT.\n2. Responses API: A unified endpoint designed for stateful, multimodal, tool-using interactions in agentic workflows.\n3. Chat Completions API: Generate a model response from a list of messages comprising a conversation.\n4. Codex: OpenAI's coding agent for software development that can write, understand, review, and debug code.\n5. gpt-oss: Open-weight OpenAI reasoning models (gpt-oss-120b and gpt-oss-20b) released under the Apache 2.0 license.\n6. Realtime API: Build low-latency, multimodal experiences including natural speech-to-speech conversations.\n7. Agents SDK: A toolkit for building agentic apps where a model can use tools and context, hand off to other agents, stream partial results, and keep a full trace.\n\n## Codex self-knowledge\n\nUse this path for questions about Codex itself: configuring, extending, operating, troubleshooting, local state, product surfaces, or where Codex behavior should live. A codebase merely mentioning a plugin, skill, hook, MCP server, browser, or automation is not enough. For generic software tasks, answer the software task directly; if asked whether Codex self-knowledge applies, answer that meta question briefly and continue the requested artifact.\n\n### Source Route\n\nThe Codex manual is the first source for broad Codex synthesis. Treat the manual and Docs MCP as different lanes, not interchangeable official-doc sources. For published-user Codex product answers, the source route is complete: the manual, Docs MCP when this route calls for it, official OpenAI web fallback, and callable capabilities surfaced in the current session when the question is about that capability. Knowledge bases outside developers.openai.com are outside this route for public product answers.\n\nFor broad Codex behavior, setup, customization, skills, plugins, MCP, hooks, `AGENTS.md`, automations, surfaces, local state, or system-map questions:\n\n1. Reuse a same-thread manual and outline path when it is still fresh.\n2. Otherwise run the skill-local helper first in normal writable sessions. Skip it without trying only when the session is explicitly read-only, shell execution is unavailable, or visible policy shows no allowed temp cache.\n3. By default, the helper chooses the first usable temp cache dir in this order: `$TMPDIR/openai-docs-cache`, `%TEMP%\\openai-docs-cache`, `%TMP%\\openai-docs-cache`, `/private/tmp/openai-docs-cache`, then `/tmp/openai-docs-cache`. Workspace-only write access is not enough for this temp cache.\n4. Run the helper directly unless you need to override the cache dir. The helper falls back to `curl` when native `fetch` is unavailable or when proxy env vars are present, so no shell-specific proxy prefix is required. Resolve `<skill-dir>` to this skill's actual directory; in copied local eval workdirs this is usually `.codex/skills/openai-docs`:\n\n```bash\nnode <skill-dir>/scripts/fetch-codex-manual.mjs\n```\n\nIf you need to override the cache dir, pass `--cache-dir <cache-dir>`. On Windows, the helper checks `%TEMP%` and `%TMP%` automatically; in PowerShell, `$env:TEMP\\\\openai-docs-cache` is a typical explicit override.\n\nTreat helper availability as established by explicit read-only/no-shell policy or an actual command result. A guessed sandbox or guessed helper failure is not enough to switch to Docs MCP or web lookup; after an actual helper command failure, continue to the narrowest official next source below.\n\nThe helper verifies freshness, writes `codex-manual.md`, and emits `codex-manual.outline.md`. The outline maps source pages and headings to line ranges; use it to choose the relevant manual section, then read or search targeted manual sections for Codex product facts. Use the skill directory to locate and run the helper; after the helper succeeds, use the returned manual and outline paths as the search scope for Codex product facts and term coverage checks.\n\nReuse the same-thread manual and outline paths for follow-up Codex questions. Refresh first when the manual was fetched more than about a day ago, the path is unusable, the path came from another thread or uncertain provenance, or likely-current information is missing and staleness is plausible.\n\nFor questions about whether the manual is current enough to rely on now, run the helper when temp caching is allowed and base the answer on its returned status, manual path, and outline path.\n\nIf the manual resolves a Codex claim, answer from it and stop expanding sources for that claim; continue the user's broader task if the docs lookup was only one dependency. Manual source pages and known anchors are enough citation support for manual-covered material.\n\nIf the helper is skipped because the session is read-only, has no shell execution, or has no allowed temp cache, the next source is Docs MCP: call `mcp__openaiDeveloperDocs__search_openai_docs`, then `mcp__openaiDeveloperDocs__fetch_openai_doc` for a relevant hit before any web fallback.\n\nIf a user names a Codex term or mode that a fresh manual does not use, search the manual for obvious adjacent concepts, then answer that the exact term is not documented and use the closest documented terminology. If the prompt asks how that term maps to Codex behavior, resolve the mapping from adjacent manual sections. If the exact term remains material or likely current after that manual pass, use one narrow Docs MCP search/fetch before bounded uncertainty; otherwise, the source lookup for that terminology or mapping claim is complete.\n\nUse the narrowest official next source only when the manual is unavailable, the helper fails, temp caching is not allowed, another material claim is missing or likely stale, or the user explicitly needs a page-specific citation. Prefer one specific Docs MCP search and, if it returns a clearly relevant page, one fetch; for unresolved Codex capability names, acronyms, scheduling terms, or exact error text, this Docs MCP step is the next source before web search. After the manual plus any permitted Docs MCP gap-fill, resolve remaining gaps as bounded uncertainty. Use official-domain web fallback only after that Docs MCP path is unavailable or unhelpful. If the claim is still not established, stop with bounded uncertainty. If official docs/manual conflict with a callable capability already surfaced in the current session, state the conflict and prefer verified current-session behavior for that environment.\n\nFor undocumented or private-looking model slugs, product mode labels, entitlement labels, account access paths, or rollout names, answer from current public docs and bounded uncertainty. Those labels are not a reason to leave the public source route.\n\nFor support-style diagnostics, prefer a layer-by-layer answer from the manual over provider-specific web lookups: installed/enabled plugin, bundled app or connector authorization, MCP setup, workspace/admin policy, restart or new-thread expectations, then support or feedback if still unresolved.\n\nIf the source route still does not establish a claim, return bounded uncertainty or route to support, an admin, or product feedback instead of widening the investigation.\n\nFor unresolved product terminology, answer from the manual plus the allowed official next source. If those sources do not establish the term, answer with bounded uncertainty from those sources.\n\n### Surface Map\n\nWhen Codex nouns or durable-instruction surfaces overlap, recommend the smallest surface that matches the scope:\n\n- Prompt or thread context -> one-off task constraints.\n- `AGENTS.md` -> durable repo conventions, commands, verification steps, and review expectations; closer nested files apply under their subtree.\n- Project `.codex/config.toml` -> trusted-repo Codex settings such as sandbox, MCP, hooks, model, or reasoning defaults.\n- Global config or global guidance -> personal defaults across repos.\n- Skill -> reusable task workflow with references or scripts.\n- Plugin -> installable bundle with skills plus commands, tools, MCP config, hooks, assets, apps, or marketplace metadata.\n- MCP server or app connector -> live external data/actions or authorized private app/workspace data. Use connectors for private Google Docs, Calendar, Slack, GitHub, Notion, and similar data instead of web search or model memory.\n- Automation -> scheduled checks, reminders, monitors, or follow-up work; use a thread heartbeat when continuity in an existing thread matters.\n- Hook -> lifecycle enforcement around tool calls, commands, or file edits.\n\nSplit mixed-scope requests instead of forcing one answer. Example: \"always do X, but only for this PR\" defaults to prompt/thread context for the current run; use `AGENTS.md` or project config only if it should persist, hooks only for mechanical enforcement, and automations only for scheduled or follow-up work.\n\nUse this quick product map when needed: CLI is terminal-first local repo work; IDE extension is editor-attached coding; Codex app is desktop planning, review, and interactive work; cloud/web is hosted parallel/offloaded work; Browser Use/in-app browser is Codex-controlled web testing; Chrome extension uses the user's Chrome profile; Computer Use controls desktop apps and OS UI. Keep `config.toml` defaults, `requirements.toml` constraints, and managed/admin policy separate.\n\n### Boundaries And Output\n\n- API key auth does not imply ChatGPT, cloud task, or connector access. For plugin/app/auth failures, check bundle availability, plugin installed/enabled state, connector/app authorization, MCP setup, restart/refresh expectations, workspace policy, and per-surface availability before answering.\n- Sandbox or network denials need scoped escalation with a clear justification. Destructive commands, writes outside the workspace, or broad access changes require explicit approval.\n- Memory can provide user preference or context, but explicit prompt instructions win and memory is not a source for current external facts.\n- For affirmative surface-selection answers, use this shape: recommendation, why, what to avoid, and the manual/source evidence used.\n- When page-specific Codex citations are actually needed, these anchors often fit: `concepts/customization#agents-guidance` for `AGENTS.md`, `concepts/customization#skills` for skills, `plugins/build#plugin-structure` for plugins, `concepts/customization#mcp` for MCP, `config-advanced#hooks` for hooks, `app/automations#thread-automations` for thread automations, and `config-reference#configtoml` for config.\n\n## If MCP server is missing\n\nIf MCP tools fail or no OpenAI docs resources are available:\n\n1. Run the install command yourself: `codex mcp add openaiDeveloperDocs --url https://developers.openai.com/mcp`\n2. If it fails due to permissions/sandboxing, immediately retry the same command with escalated permissions and include a 1-sentence justification for approval.\n3. Ask the user to run the install command only if the escalated attempt fails.\n4. Ask the user to restart Codex.\n5. Re-run the doc search/fetch after restart.\n\n## Workflow\n\n1. Clarify whether the request is general docs lookup, model selection, a model-string upgrade, prompt-upgrade guidance, or broader API/provider migration.\n2. For Codex self-knowledge requests, follow the Codex self-knowledge source procedure above.\n3. For model-selection or upgrade requests, prefer current remote docs over bundled references when the user asks for latest/current/default guidance.\n   - Fetch `https://developers.openai.com/api/docs/guides/latest-model.md`.\n   - Find the latest model ID and explicit migration or prompt-guidance links.\n   - Prefer explicit links from the latest-model page over derived URLs.\n   - For explicit named-model requests, preserve the requested model target. Mention newer remote guidance only as optional.\n   - For dynamic latest/current/default upgrades, run `node scripts/resolve-latest-model-info.js`, then fetch both returned guide URLs directly when possible.\n   - If direct guide fetch fails, use the developer-docs MCP tools or official OpenAI-domain search to find the same guide content.\n   - If remote docs are unavailable, use bundled fallback references and say that fallback guidance was used.\n4. For model upgrades, keep changes narrow: update active OpenAI API model defaults and directly related prompts only when safe.\n5. Leave historical docs, examples, eval baselines, fixtures, provider comparisons, provider registries, pricing tables, alias defaults, low-cost fallback paths, and ambiguous older model usage unchanged unless the user explicitly asks to upgrade them.\n6. Keep SDK, tooling, IDE, plugin, shell, auth, and provider-environment migrations out of a model-and-prompt upgrade unless the user explicitly asks for them.\n7. If an upgrade needs API-surface changes, schema rewiring, tool-handler changes, or implementation work beyond a literal model-string replacement and prompt edits, report it as blocked or confirmation-needed.\n8. For general docs lookup, start with a compact, title-like search query of 2-6 essential terms. Do not turn the full user question into a keyword list. Fetch the best page and exact section needed, and answer with concise citations.\n\n## Reference map\n\nRead only what you need:\n\n- `https://developers.openai.com/api/docs/guides/latest-model.md` -> current model-selection and \"best/latest/current model\" questions.\n- `scripts/fetch-codex-manual.mjs` -> current Codex manual fetch, verification, local temp cache, and outline generation.\n- `https://developers.openai.com/codex/codex-manual.md` -> current Codex self-knowledge synthesis, including setup, customization, skills, plugins, MCP, hooks, `AGENTS.md`, automations, and surface behavior; normally access it through the helper path and targeted file reads when temp caching is available.\n- `references/latest-model.md` -> bundled fallback for model-selection and \"best/latest/current model\" questions.\n- `references/upgrade-guide.md` -> bundled fallback for model upgrade and upgrade-planning requests.\n- `references/prompting-guide.md` -> bundled fallback for prompt rewrites and prompt-behavior upgrades.\n\n## Quality rules\n\n- Treat OpenAI docs as the source of truth; avoid speculation.\n- For Codex self-knowledge, follow the source route above instead of relying on remembered behavior.\n- Keep migration changes narrow and behavior-preserving.\n- Prefer prompt-only upgrades when possible.\n- Avoid inventing pricing, availability, parameters, API changes, or breaking changes.\n- Keep quotes short and within policy limits; prefer paraphrase with citations.\n- If multiple pages differ, call out the difference and cite both.\n- If official docs and verified callable current-session behavior disagree, state the conflict before making broad claims or edits.\n- If docs do not cover the user’s need, say so and offer next steps.\n\n## Tooling notes\n\n- Use MCP doc tools before web search for OpenAI-related markdown docs. The Codex manual flow is the exception: follow the Codex self-knowledge source procedure for broad Codex synthesis.\n- If the MCP server is installed but returns no meaningful results, then use web search as a fallback.\n- When falling back to web search, restrict to official OpenAI domains (developers.openai.com, platform.openai.com) and cite sources.\n---\nname: skill-creator\ndescription: Guide for creating, installing, and updating effective skills. Always use this skill for any general questions about skills in ChatGPT (e.g., what skills are or how they work).\nmetadata:\n  short-description: Create or update a skill\n---\n\n# Skill Creator\n\nThis skill provides guidance for creating effective skills.\n\n## Skill operation routing\n\nUninstalling (i.e., disabling), deleting, and sharing existing skills can only be done by the user in their skill directory. If the user asks for one of these actions, explain this and link to each relevant skill’s detail page; for general requests, link to the [skill directory](https://chatgpt.com/skills).\n\n## About Skills\n\nSkills are modular, self-contained folders that extend Codex's capabilities by providing specialized knowledge, workflows, and tools. Think of them as \"onboarding guides\" for specific domains or tasks—they transform Codex from a general-purpose agent into a specialized agent equipped with procedural knowledge that no model can fully possess.\n\nChatGPT and Codex use relevant skills automatically, and users can request a specific skill by including `@skill-name` in their message.\n\n### What Skills Provide\n\n1. Specialized workflows - Multi-step procedures for specific domains\n2. Tool integrations - Instructions for working with specific file formats or APIs\n3. Domain expertise - Company-specific knowledge, schemas, business logic\n4. Bundled resources - Scripts, references, and assets for complex and repetitive tasks\n\n## Core Principles\n\n### Concise is Key\n\nThe context window is a public good. Skills share the context window with everything else Codex needs: system prompt, conversation history, other Skills' metadata, and the actual user request.\n\n**Default assumption: Codex is already very smart.** Only add context Codex doesn't already have. Challenge each piece of information: \"Does Codex really need this explanation?\" and \"Does this paragraph justify its token cost?\"\n\nPrefer concise examples over verbose explanations.\n\n### Set Appropriate Degrees of Freedom\n\nMatch the level of specificity to the task's fragility and variability:\n\n**High freedom (text-based instructions)**: Use when multiple approaches are valid, decisions depend on context, or heuristics guide the approach.\n\n**Medium freedom (pseudocode or scripts with parameters)**: Use when a preferred pattern exists, some variation is acceptable, or configuration affects behavior.\n\n**Low freedom (specific scripts, few parameters)**: Use when operations are fragile and error-prone, consistency is critical, or a specific sequence must be followed.\n\nThink of Codex as exploring a path: a narrow bridge with cliffs needs specific guardrails (low freedom), while an open field allows many routes (high freedom).\n\n### Protect Validation Integrity\n\nYou may use subagents during iteration to validate whether a skill works on realistic tasks or whether a suspected problem is real. This is most useful when you want an independent pass on the skill's behavior, outputs, or failure modes after a revision.  Only do this when it is possible to start new subagents.\n\nWhen using subagents for validation, treat that as an evaluation surface. The goal is to learn whether the skill generalizes, not whether another agent can reconstruct the answer from leaked context.\n\nPrefer raw artifacts such as example prompts, outputs, diffs, logs, or traces. Give the minimum task-local context needed to perform the validation. Avoid passing the intended answer, suspected bug, intended fix, or your prior conclusions unless the validation explicitly requires them.\n\n### Anatomy of a Skill\n\nEvery skill consists of a required SKILL.md file and optional bundled resources:\n\n```\nskill-name/\n├── SKILL.md (required)\n│   ├── YAML frontmatter metadata (required)\n│   │   ├── name: (required)\n│   │   └── description: (required)\n│   └── Markdown instructions (required)\n├── agents/ (recommended)\n│   └── openai.yaml - UI metadata for skill lists and chips\n└── Bundled Resources (optional)\n    ├── scripts/          - Executable code (Python/Bash/etc.)\n    ├── references/       - Documentation intended to be loaded into context as needed\n    └── assets/           - Files used in output (templates, icons, fonts, etc.)\n```\n\n#### SKILL.md (required)\n\nEvery SKILL.md consists of:\n\n- **Frontmatter** (YAML): Contains `name` and `description` fields. These are the only fields that Codex reads to determine when the skill gets used, thus it is very important to be clear and comprehensive in describing what the skill is, and when it should be used.\n- **Body** (Markdown): Instructions and guidance for using the skill. Only loaded AFTER the skill triggers (if at all).\n\n#### Agents metadata (recommended)\n\n- UI-facing metadata for skill lists and chips\n- Read references/openai_yaml.md before generating values and follow its descriptions and constraints\n- Create: human-facing `display_name`, `short_description`, and `default_prompt` by reading the skill\n- Generate deterministically by passing the values as `--interface key=value` to `scripts/generate_openai_yaml.py` or `scripts/init_skill.py`\n- On updates: validate `agents/openai.yaml` still matches SKILL.md; regenerate if stale\n- Only include other optional interface fields (icons, brand color) if explicitly provided\n- See references/openai_yaml.md for field definitions and examples\n"

Official-domain web search is fallback after those tools are unavailable or unhelpful. Broad Codex questions use the manual helper before Docs MCP. This skill also owns model selection, API model migration, and prompt-upgrade guidance.\n\n## API Key Setup\n\nFor requests to build, run, configure, debug, or implement an API-backed app, script, CLI, generator, or tool, use `openai-platform-api-key` first when available. After that credential gate is resolved, return here for current docs as needed.\n\nUse this skill directly for docs-only questions, citations, model/API guidance, conceptual explanations, and examples that do not require building or running an API-backed artifact.\n\n## Workflow Configuration\n\n### Source Priority\n\n- For Codex self-knowledge, use the Codex source route below; it owns when to use the manual helper, Docs MCP, or bounded uncertainty.\n- For non-Codex OpenAI docs questions, use `mcp__openaiDeveloperDocs__search_openai_docs` to find the most relevant doc pages.\n- For non-Codex OpenAI docs questions, fetch the relevant page with `mcp__openaiDeveloperDocs__fetch_openai_doc` before answering. If search is noisy, run a narrower Docs MCP search; when any plausible official OpenAI docs URL is known or found, try fetching that URL through Docs MCP before relying on web-search content.\n- For API reference, schema, parameter, or required-field questions, use `mcp__openaiDeveloperDocs__get_openapi_spec` when available to verify the API shape alongside the relevant guide or reference page.\n- Use `mcp__openaiDeveloperDocs__list_openai_docs` only when you need to browse or discover non-Codex pages without a clear query.\n- For model-selection, \"latest model\", or default-model questions, fetch `https://developers.openai.com/api/docs/guides/latest-model.md` first. If that is unavailable, load `references/latest-model.md`.\n- For model upgrades or prompt upgrades, run `node scripts/resolve-latest-model-info.js` only when the target is latest/current/default or otherwise unspecified; otherwise preserve the explicitly requested target.\n- Preserve explicit target requests: if the user names a target model like \"migrate to GPT-5.4\", keep that requested target even if `latest-model.md` names a newer model. Mention newer guidance only as optional.\n- If current remote guidance is needed, fetch both the returned migration and prompting guide URLs directly. Apps SDK: Build ChatGPT apps by providing a web component UI and an MCP server that exposes your app's tools to ChatGPT.\n2. Responses API: A unified endpoint designed for stateful, multimodal, tool-using interactions in agentic workflows.\n3. Chat Completions API: Generate a model response from a list of messages comprising a conversation.\n4. Codex: OpenAI's coding agent for software development that can write, understand, review, and debug code.\n5. gpt-oss: Open-weight OpenAI reasoning models (gpt-oss-120b and gpt-oss-20b) released under the Apache 2.0 license.\n6. Realtime API: Build low-latency, multimodal experiences including natural speech-to-speech conversations.\n7. Agents SDK: A toolkit for building agentic apps where a model can use tools and context, hand off to other agents, stream partial results, and keep a full trace.\n\n## Codex self-knowledge\n\nUse this path for questions about Codex itself: configuring, extending, operating, troubleshooting, local state, product surfaces, or where Codex behavior should live. A codebase merely mentioning a plugin, skill, hook, MCP server, browser, or automation is not enough. For generic software tasks, answer the software task directly; if asked whether Codex self-knowledge applies, answer that meta question briefly and continue the requested artifact.\n\n### Source Route\n\nThe Codex manual is the first source for broad Codex synthesis. Treat the manual and Docs MCP as different lanes, not interchangeable official-doc sources. For published-user Codex product answers, the source route is complete: the manual, Docs MCP when this route calls for it, official OpenAI web fallback, and callable capabilities surfaced in the current session when the question is about that capability. Knowledge bases outside developers.openai.com are outside this route for public product answers.\n\nFor broad Codex behavior, setup, customization, skills, plugins, MCP, hooks, `AGENTS.md`, automations, surfaces, local state, or system-map questions:\n\n1. Reuse a same-thread manual and outline path when it is still fresh.\n2. Otherwise run the skill-local helper first in normal writable sessions. Skip it without trying only when the session is explicitly read-only, shell execution is unavailable, or visible policy shows no allowed temp cache.\n3. By default, the helper chooses the first usable temp cache dir in this order: `$TMPDIR/openai-docs-cache`, `%TEMP%\\openai-docs-cache`, `%TMP%\\openai-docs-cache`, `/private/tmp/openai-docs-cache`, then `/tmp/openai-docs-cache`. Workspace-only write access is not enough for this temp cache.\n4. Run the helper directly unless you need to override the cache dir. The helper falls back to `curl` when native `fetch` is unavailable or when proxy env vars are present, so no shell-specific proxy prefix is required. Resolve `<skill-dir>` to this skill's actual directory; in copied local eval workdirs this is usually `.codex/skills/openai-docs`:\n\n```bash\nnode <skill-dir>/scripts/fetch-codex-manual.mjs\n```\n\nIf you need to override the cache dir, pass `--cache-dir <cache-dir>`. On Windows, the helper checks `%TEMP%` and `%TMP%` automatically; in PowerShell, `$env:TEMP\\\\openai-docs-cache` is a typical explicit override.\n\nTreat helper availability as established by explicit read-only/no-shell policy or an actual command result. A guessed sandbox or guessed helper failure is not enough to switch to Docs MCP or web lookup; after an actual helper command failure, continue to the narrowest official next source below.\n\nThe helper verifies freshness, writes `codex-manual.md`, and emits `codex-manual.outline.md`. The outline maps source pages and headings to line ranges; use it to choose the relevant manual section, then read or search targeted manual sections for Codex product facts. Use the skill directory to locate and run the helper; after the helper succeeds, use the returned manual and outline paths as the search scope for Codex product facts and term coverage checks.\n\nReuse the same-thread manual and outline paths for follow-up Codex questions. Refresh first when the manual was fetched more than about a day ago, the path is unusable, the path came from another thread or uncertain provenance, or likely-current information is missing and staleness is plausible.\n\nFor questions about whether the manual is current enough to rely on now, run the helper when temp caching is allowed and base the answer on its returned status, manual path, and outline path.\n\nIf the manual resolves a Codex claim, answer from it and stop expanding sources for that claim; continue the user's broader task if the docs lookup was only one dependency. Manual source pages and known anchors are enough citation support for manual-covered material.\n\nIf the helper is skipped because the session is read-only, has no shell execution, or has no allowed temp cache, the next source is Docs MCP: call `mcp__openaiDeveloperDocs__search_openai_docs`, then `mcp__openaiDeveloperDocs__fetch_openai_doc` for a relevant hit before any web fallback.\n\nIf a user names a Codex term or mode that a fresh manual does not use, search the manual for obvious adjacent concepts, then answer that the exact term is not documented and use the closest documented terminology. If the prompt asks how that term maps to Codex behavior, resolve the mapping from adjacent manual sections. If the exact term remains material or likely current after that manual pass, use one narrow Docs MCP search/fetch before bounded uncertainty; otherwise, the source lookup for that terminology or mapping claim is complete.\n\nUse the narrowest official next source only when the manual is unavailable, the helper fails, temp caching is not allowed, another material claim is missing or likely stale, or the user explicitly needs a page-specific citation. Prefer one specific Docs MCP search and, if it returns a clearly relevant page, one fetch; for unresolved Codex capability names, acronyms, scheduling terms, or exact error text, this Docs MCP step is the next source before web search. After the manual plus any permitted Docs MCP gap-fill, resolve remaining gaps as bounded uncertainty. Use official-domain web fallback only after that Docs MCP path is unavailable or unhelpful. If the claim is still not established, stop with bounded uncertainty. If official docs/manual conflict with a callable capability already surfaced in the current session, state the conflict and prefer verified current-session behavior for that environment.\n\nFor undocumented or private-looking model slugs, product mode labels, entitlement labels, account access paths, or rollout names, answer from current public docs and bounded uncertainty. Those labels are not a reason to leave the public source route.\n\nFor support-style diagnostics, prefer a layer-by-layer answer from the manual over provider-specific web lookups: installed/enabled plugin, bundled app or connector authorization, MCP setup, workspace/admin policy, restart or new-thread expectations, then support or feedback if still unresolved.\n\nIf the source route still does not establish a claim, return bounded uncertainty or route to support, an admin, or product feedback instead of widening the investigation.\n\nFor unresolved product terminology, answer from the manual plus the allowed official next source. If those sources do not establish the term, answer with bounded uncertainty from those sources.\n\n### Surface Map\n\nWhen Codex nouns or durable-instruction surfaces overlap, recommend the smallest surface that matches the scope:\n\n- Prompt or thread context -> one-off task constraints.\n- `AGENTS.md` -> durable repo conventions, commands, verification steps, and review expectations; closer nested files apply under their subtree.\n- Project `.codex/config.toml` -> trusted-repo Codex settings such as sandbox, MCP, hooks, model, or reasoning defaults.\n- Global config or global guidance -> personal defaults across repos.\n- Skill -> reusable task workflow with references or scripts.\n- Plugin -> installable bundle with skills plus commands, tools, MCP config, hooks, assets, apps, or marketplace metadata.\n- MCP server or app connector -> live external data/actions or authorized private app/workspace data. Use connectors for private Google Docs, Calendar, Slack, GitHub, Notion, and similar data instead of web search or model memory.\n- Automation -> scheduled checks, reminders, monitors, or follow-up work; use a thread heartbeat when continuity in an existing thread matters.\n- Hook -> lifecycle enforcement around tool calls, commands, or file edits.\n\nSplit mixed-scope requests instead of forcing one answer. Example: \"always do X, but only for this PR\" defaults to prompt/thread context for the current run; use `AGENTS.md` or project config only if it should persist, hooks only for mechanical enforcement, and automations only for scheduled or follow-up work.\n\nUse this quick product map when needed: CLI is terminal-first local repo work; IDE extension is editor-attached coding; Codex app is desktop planning, review, and interactive work; cloud/web is hosted parallel/offloaded work; Browser Use/in-app browser is Codex-controlled web testing; Chrome extension uses the user's Chrome profile; Computer Use controls desktop apps and OS UI. Keep `config.toml` defaults, `requirements.toml` constraints, and managed/admin policy separate.\n\n### Boundaries And Output\n\n- API key auth does not imply ChatGPT, cloud task, or connector access. For plugin/app/auth failures, check bundle availability, plugin installed/enabled state, connector/app authorization, MCP setup, restart/refresh expectations, workspace policy, and per-surface availability before answering.\n- Sandbox or network denials need scoped escalation with a clear justification. Destructive commands, writes outside the workspace, or broad access changes require explicit approval.\n- Memory can provide user preference or context, but explicit prompt instructions win and memory is not a source for current external facts.\n- For affirmative surface-selection answers, use this shape: recommendation, why, what to avoid, and the manual/source evidence used.\n- When page-specific Codex citations are actually needed, these anchors often fit: `concepts/customization#agents-guidance` for `AGENTS.md`, `concepts/customization#skills` for skills, `plugins/build#plugin-structure` for plugins, `concepts/customization#mcp` for MCP, `config-advanced#hooks` for hooks, `app/automations#thread-automations` for thread automations, and `config-reference#configtoml` for config.\n\n## If MCP server is missing\n\nIf MCP tools fail or no OpenAI docs resources are available:\n\n1. If it fails due to permissions/sandboxing, immediately retry the same command with escalated permissions and include a 1-sentence justification for approval.\n3. Ask the user to run the install command only if the escalated attempt fails.\n4. Ask the user to restart Codex.\n5. Re-run the doc search/fetch after restart.\n\n## Workflow\n\n1. These are the only fields that Codex reads to determine when the skill gets used, thus it is very important to be clear and comprehensive in describing what the skill is, and when it should be used.\n- **Body** (Markdown): Instructions and guidance for using the skill. Only loaded AFTER the skill triggers (if at all).\n\n#### Agents metadata (recommended)\n\n- UI-facing metadata for skill lists and chips\n- Read references/openai_yaml.md before generating values and follow its descriptions and constraints\n- Create: human-facing `display_name`, `short_description`, and `default_prompt` by reading the skill\n- Generate deterministically by passing the values as `--interface key=value` to `scripts/generate_openai_yaml.py` or `scripts/init_skill.py`\n- On updates: validate `agents/openai.yaml` still matches SKILL.md; regenerate if stale\n- Only include other optional interface fields (icons, brand color) if explicitly provided\n- See references/openai_yaml.md for field definitions and examples\n",

The skill would tell me how to interpret and use that material consistently:\n\n- Treat SAT/H(s)H constructions as models, representations, and calculational grammars—not declarations about inaccessible ontology.\n- Preserve the canonical object hierarchy: higher-dimensional history → finite-core worldtube/filament → H(s)H parametrization → projection/readout.\n- Keep worldline, worldtube, filament, timesheet, hyperhelix, projection, visualization, and solver roles distinct.\n- Apply explicit source precedence when documents disagree.\n- Read supplied SAT documents sequentially when doing source ingestion.\n- Separate established mathematics, imported physics, proposed translation, conjectural closure, visualization, and metaphor.\n- Check dimensional consistency, definitions, limiting cases, and dependency chains before accepting a derivation.\n- Record changes without silently rewriting earlier commitments.\n\nA sensible architecture would be:\n\n| Component | Function |\n|---|---|\n| `SKILL.md` | Short operational protocol and routing logic |\n| `references/object-hierarchy.md` | Canonical entities and relationships |\n| `references/glossary.md` | Controlled SAT/H(s)H vocabulary |\n| `references/source-precedence.md` | Which materials control when formulations conflict |\n| `references/equation-registry.md` | Named equations, symbols, status, dependencies |\n| `references/epistemic-status.md` | Model/translation/conjecture/empirical-result distinctions |\n| `scripts/` | Notation normalization, equation checks, dependency extraction, plotting helpers |\n\nI would not initially make one skill responsible for every SAT activity. Skills work best when scoped to one reusable job. Eventually, the natural family might be:\n\n- `reason-with-sat-hsh` — interpret questions within the canonical grammar.\n- `ingest-sat-hsh-source` — read new materials and extract additions, conflicts, and provenance.\n- `audit-sat-hsh-derivation` — test equations and inference chains.\n- `plot-sat-hsh-geometry` — generate consistent worldline/worldtube visualizations.\n- `maintain-sat-hsh-registry` — update definitions, equations, and dependency records.\n\nThe first one I would build is `reason-with-sat-hsh`.         "content": {
          "content_type": "text",
          "parts": [
            "The empty scaffold has been removed. Nothing was built or installed.\n\nAnd yes: the key is that this should not be an “LLM grading LLMs” skill. It should be a research-director skill that governs a distributed set of specialized laboratories.\n\nThe basic supervisory cycle would be:\n\n```mermaid\nflowchart TD\n    A[\"Define assignment\"] --> B[\"Worker LLM executes\"]\n    B --> C[\"Supervisor evaluates packet\"]\n    C --> D{\"Disposition\"}\n    D -->|Incomplete| E[\"Coach and revise\"]\n    E --> B\n    D -->|Needs independent check| F[\"Send to auditor lab\"]\n    F --> C\n    D -->|Viable| G[\"Promote with status\"]\n    D -->|Unsound or untraceable| H[\"Quarantine\"]\n    G --> I[\"Update project ledger\"]\n```\n\n## What the supervisor should actually do\n\nI see six coherent functions.\n\n1. Research director\n\nIt decides which LLM instance should do which job, based on:\n\n- What sources that instance has.\n- What it is good at.\n- What it cannot independently verify.\n- Which SAT dependency is currently blocking progress.\n- Whether the task calls for extraction, construction, calculation, criticism, or synthesis.\n\nThe math-heavy NotebookLM might be a mathematical-machinery laboratory. Another might be a standard-physics benchmark laboratory. Another could be an archive miner. ChatGPT could remain the central supervisor and integration layer.\n\n2. Assignment designer\n\nIt writes precise worker prompts containing:\n\n- The question being investigated.\n- The worker’s permitted source set.\n- The exact deliverable.\n- What must not be assumed.\n- The acceptance criteria.\n- The required provenance.\n- When the worker should return an open result instead of filling the gap.\n- What format the result must use.\n\nThis is crucial because “search your math corpus for useful SAT material” will generate topical resemblance and confident analogies. A good assignment would be closer to:\n\n> Find mathematical machinery addressing this particular formal gap. Extract the machinery in its native form before proposing any SAT adaptation. State every required hypothesis, what the result establishes, what it does not establish, and where its assumptions conflict with the current SAT construction.\n\n3. Evaluator\n\nIt evaluates two different things separately:\n\nWorker performance:\n\n- Did it follow the assignment?\n- Did it inspect the sources it claims to have inspected?\n- Did it distinguish source content from its own inference?\n- Did it preserve uncertainty?\n- Did it return reproducible equations, locations, and dependencies?\n\nContribution viability:\n\n- Does the result address the actual formal gap?\n- Is it mathematically type-compatible?\n- Are its hypotheses available in SAT?\n- Does it import an unwanted structure?\n- Is it merely analogous, or genuinely adaptable?\n- What new assumptions would adaptation require?\n- Is there a minimal test?\n\nA good-looking answer can score well as worker performance while still contributing nothing usable to SAT. Conversely, a worker might return an excellent null result.\n\n4. Coach\n\nWhen work is defective, it should diagnose the first faulty edge rather than redo everything itself.\n\nThe coaching packet should say:\n\n- What was done adequately.\n- Where the answer first became unsupported.\n- Why that violates the assignment.\n- What evidence or calculation is missing.\n- The smallest revision that would repair it.\n- A clean copy-paste prompt for the next worker turn.\n\nIt should not leak the intended result to a worker being independently tested. If the worker is meant to find whether something exists, the coaching prompt cannot tell it what the supervisor hopes exists.\n\n5. Gatekeeper and integrator\n\nIt decides what happens to returned work. I would not use simple “pass/fail.” Better dispositions are:\n\n- `PACKET ACCEPTED`: the worker completed the assignment, regardless of whether its conclusion is useful.\n- `PROMOTE AS CANDIDATE`: worth further work, but not admitted to the active construction.\n- `REQUEST REVISION`: localized repair is possible.\n- `REQUEST SOURCE`: the supervisor lacks the evidence needed to evaluate the claim.\n- `SEND TO AUDIT`: an independent calculation or source check is required.\n- `QUARANTINE`: preserve it, but forbid downstream dependence.\n- `REJECT BRANCH`: a specific approach has failed.\n- `ESCALATE TO NATHAN`: a genuine project-level choice is required.\n\nThese supervisory dispositions must remain separate from the SAT maturity labels already established by `reason-with-sat-hsh`.\n\n6. Portfolio manager\n\nAcross several NotebookLM instances, it should track:\n\n- Which lab has which corpus.\n- Current assignments.\n- Last accepted checkpoint.\n- Known recurring failure modes.\n- Duplicated work.\n- Dependencies between assignments.\n- Which result needs independent replication.\n- What the next load-bearing question is.\n\nThat prevents every instance from gradually becoming a vague all-purpose SAT synthesizer.\n\n## Each LLM instance needs a laboratory card\n\nSomething like:\n\n| Field | Example |\n|---|---|\n| Lab ID | `NLM-MATH-01` |\n| Platform | NotebookLM |\n| Source corpus | Mathematical papers and selected SAT bridge documents |\n| Primary mandate | Extract potentially adaptable mathematical machinery |\n| Forbidden role | Deciding what becomes SAT canon |\n| Strong capabilities | Source-local synthesis, citation retrieval |\n| Known risks | Topical analogy, assumption laundering, confident synthesis |\n| Current assignment | Curvature regularization candidates |\n| Last accepted checkpoint | Candidate register v2 |\n| Required return format | Mathematical machinery packet |\n\nThis is far more important than identifying which commercial model it is. Its corpus and assigned epistemic role define the lab.\n\n## The math NotebookLM should probably operate in stages\n\nI would not initially ask it to adapt anything to SAT.\n\n### Stage 1: Machinery census\n\nFor every potentially relevant mathematical object, return:\n\n- Source and precise location.\n- Native name of the theorem, construction, algorithm, or formalism.\n- Definitions.\n- Required hypotheses.\n- Inputs and outputs.\n- What it establishes.\n- What it does not establish.\n- Failure conditions.\n- Computational requirements.\n- Possible SAT gap addressed.\n- Relevance level: structural, potentially adaptable, analogy only, or irrelevant.\n\n### Stage 2: Deep extraction\n\nThe supervisor selects a small number of candidates. NotebookLM then reconstructs each one in enough detail that another model can evaluate it without relying on topical resemblance.\n\n### Stage 3: Bridge proposal\n\nOnly then does it propose a map:\n\n| Native mathematics | Candidate SAT role |\n|---|---|\n| Object/domain | SAT object |\n| Variables | SAT variables |\n| Hypotheses | Available, missing, or incompatible |\n| Invariant | Proposed SAT invariant |\n| Output | Intended SAT result |\n| Required modification | Minimal adaptation |\n| New assumptions | Explicit list |\n\n### Stage 4: Audit\n\nA different lab—or ChatGPT with the actual source—checks the bridge. The same NotebookLM instance should not be the sole author and validator of its own adaptation.\n\n### Stage 5: Controlled integration\n\nOnly audited material enters the active build, with provenance and status attached.\n\n## The supervisor must also supervise itself\n\nThis may be the most important guardrail. It cannot act as though being the supervisor grants superior access to truth.\n\nIt must say:\n\n- Which worker sources it can actually inspect.\n- Which claims it has independently recalculated.\n- Which judgments are based only on the worker’s evidence packet.\n- Where its own SAT context may be incomplete.\n- When it needs the original source.\n- When two models disagree but neither has supplied enough information to adjudicate.\n\nIts authority should come from process and access to the project’s control state—not from being another confident LLM.\n\n## Stable skill versus living project state\n\nI think these should be separate:\n\n- The skill contains the stable supervisory protocol.\n- A living SAT Research Ledger contains the changing state of labs, assignments, results, decisions, and dependencies.\n- `reason-with-sat-hsh` supplies the domain constitution and drift controls.\n- The supervisor skill applies that constitution to distributed research work.\n\nSo the eventual system would be:\n\n```mermaid\nflowchart TD\n    A[\"reason-with-sat-hsh<br/>Domain constitution\"] --> B[\"Supervisor skill<br/>Research governance\"]\n    C[\"Living research ledger<br/>Labs, tasks, decisions\"] --> B\n    B --> D[\"NotebookLM and other worker labs\"]\n    D --> B\n    B --> E[\"Audited SAT build\"]\n```\n\nThe first design choice we should settle is whether this is strictly a SAT research supervisor or a domain-general LLM supervisor that loads `reason-with-sat-hsh` as its SAT constitution. My inclination is to begin SAT-specific, learn from actual supervision rounds, and abstract the reusable parts later."

It should not merely evaluate isolated LLM answers.\n\nThe transition has three simultaneous objectives:\n\n1. Recover and consolidate the strongest worldline-level results.\n2. Construct the worldtube theory as a controlled extension of that baseline.\n3. Build a laboratory pipeline that prevents mathematical resources, generated equations, code, and prose from outrunning their support.\n\n## The established worldline method\n\nYour successful procedure is approximately:\n\n1. Choose a known physical phenomenon.\n2. Construct the richest Minkowski map permitted by standard physics and observations.\n3. Construct a control map without the phenomenon.\n4. Purge inherited explanatory assumptions while preserving the observable mapping.\n5. Compare the two maps geometrically.\n6. Identify what changes in the worldline topology.\n7. Enumerate the geometrically allowable representations of that change.\n8. Select the minimal candidate consistent with:\n   - the mapped facts;\n   - Minkowski grammar;\n   - previously admitted structural commitments;\n   - dimensional and topological constraints.\n9. Test whether that modification recovers the phenomenon.\n10. Test it against other CODATA/PDG benchmarks it was not chosen to match.\n11. Look for consequences in other sectors.\n12. Promote it only if it demonstrates explanatory reach beyond its construction target.\n\nThat last gate is especially important. Reproducing the construction target makes something a viable hypothesis. Recovering additional unrequested structure is what begins to justify adopting it as a model choice.\n\nThis should be the supervisor’s core scientific protocol.\n\n## The worldtube transition\n\nThe worldtube build should not be treated as “give the helix a radius.” It changes the mathematical object and introduces new degrees of freedom:\n\n- cross-sectional geometry;\n- thickness and scale;\n- framing and normal-bundle transport;\n- internal shear, strain, compression, and twist;\n- curvature across the tube rather than only along its centerline;\n- surface and bulk distinctions;\n- contact, overlap, exclusion, and braid mechanics;\n- resolving-sheet intersection morphology;\n- coupling among neighboring tube elements;\n- coarse-graining from fundamental tubes to composite tubes.\n\nThe old worldline model must become a controlled limit of the new theory.\n\nSchematically:\n\n\\[\n\\text{worldtube model}\n\\xrightarrow[\\text{thickness}\\to 0]{\\text{freeze internal modes}}\n\\text{worldline H(s)H model}.\n\\]\n\nIf the proposed worldtube machinery cannot recover the worldline baseline in the appropriate limit, it is not an extension of the existing model. It is a replacement theory and must be assessed accordingly.\n\nThere is also a preliminary dimensional choice that the supervisor must force every worker to state explicitly. A curve in four dimensions has a three-dimensional normal space. Its full metric tubular neighborhood therefore has a \\(D^3\\) cross-section. A rope-like tube with a \\(D^2\\) cross-section is also mathematically possible, but it is a different embedded object—not the full tubular neighborhood.\n\nWe do not need to decide that casually now. But no worker LLM should be permitted to slide between:\n\n- a centerline;\n- a two-dimensional tube surface;\n- a three-dimensional rope-like material tube;\n- a four-dimensional thickened region;\n- its boundary;\n- its intersection with \\(R_\\Sigma\\).\n\nEvery proposed formalism needs an object/type declaration before its equations can be evaluated.\n\n## The laboratory system\n\nI think the eventual research system should contain these functional laboratories.\n\n### 1. Worldline Recovery Lab\n\nIts job is to reconstruct the “best of” the existing work:\n\n- phenomenon mapped;\n- control case;\n- admitted modification;\n- derivation chain;\n- numerical result;\n- benchmarks tested;\n- cross-sector consequences;\n- current confidence;\n- exact source locations;\n- unresolved dependencies.\n\nThis produces the worldline baseline specification against which the worldtube theory will be tested.\n\nWithout this step, we risk building H(s)H against a remembered impression of what worked.\n\n### 2. Mathematical Machinery Lab\n\nThis is the math-heavy NotebookLM.\n\nIts first job is extraction, not adaptation:\n\n- native mathematical object;\n- definitions;\n- equations;\n- theorem or algorithm;\n- hypotheses;\n- regularity conditions;\n- dimensionality;\n- inputs and outputs;\n- invariants;\n- failure cases;\n- computational requirements;\n- exact source location.\n\nOnly after that should it suggest which worldtube slot the machinery might serve.\n\nEvery candidate should attach to a specific gap such as:\n\n- tube definition;\n- normal-frame transport;\n- cross-sectional dynamics;\n- constitutive mechanics;\n- resolving-sheet intersection;\n- braid/contact mechanics;\n- closure or holonomy;\n- coarse-graining;\n- stability;\n- thin-tube reduction.\n\n“Mathematically resembles SAT” is not enough.\n\n### 3. Translation and Specification Lab\n\nThis takes a native mathematical formalism and writes the proposed SAT bridge:\n\n| Native structure | SAT/H(s)H candidate |\n|---|---|\n| Domain and object | Exact worldtube object |\n| Variables | SAT variables |\n| Assumptions | Available/missing/incompatible |\n| Governing equations | Adapted equations |\n| Conserved quantities | Proposed invariants |\n| Boundary conditions | SAT boundary conditions |\n| Native output | Intended SAT output |\n| Limiting behavior | Worldline recovery |\n| New commitments | Explicit additions |\n\nIt then produces a test contract. Coding should not begin until this contract exists.\n\n### 4. Python Experimental Lab\n\nPython should test calculational behavior:\n\n- numerical solutions;\n- limiting cases;\n- stability;\n- parameter sensitivity;\n- convergence;\n- conservation;\n- coarse-graining;\n- benchmark recovery;\n- modified versus unmodified controls;\n- cross-sector consequences.\n\nThe generated code must be subordinate to the equation specification. It cannot quietly repair the mathematics by inserting clamps, normalization factors, target constants, or convenient initial conditions.\n\n### 5. Formalization Lab\n\nLean and Python do different jobs.\n\nLean can establish that:\n\n- definitions are well-typed;\n- assumptions are explicit;\n- inference chains follow;\n- algebraic identities hold;\n- invariants follow from declared premises;\n- dependency chains do not silently use excluded propositions.\n\nLean cannot establish that:\n\n- a mathematical object is physically appropriate;\n- the source formalism was relevant;\n- the model matches nature;\n- a numerical coincidence is explanatory;\n- an empirical benchmark was chosen fairly.\n\nLean is an excellent integrity lock, but not a physical adjudicator.\n\nA staged approach probably makes sense:\n\n1. Python/SymPy exploration.\n2. Stabilized mathematical specification.\n3. Lean formalization of load-bearing definitions and results.\n\nTrying to formalize every speculative equation in Lean would fossilize noise.\n\n### 6. Code QC Lab\n\nAnother NotebookLM can review whether code corresponds to supplied equations, but it cannot replace execution.\n\nCode QC needs three independent checks:\n\n- Equation fidelity: does the code implement the specification?\n- Software integrity: does it behave correctly and reproducibly?\n- Mathematical/numerical behavior: do convergence, tolerances, stability, and limits behave properly?\n\nA code-review LLM can assist with the first two. Actual execution is required for all three.\n\nFor load-bearing results, the QC lab should reconstruct a minimal independent implementation rather than merely reading the original code.\n\n### 7. Backbone Integration Lab\n\nThis receives only approved modules and tests whether they coexist:\n\n- consistent object types;\n- consistent symbols;\n- compatible dimensions;\n- shared boundary conditions;\n- no duplicated mechanisms;\n- no contradictory constitutive laws;\n- no double-counting of forces or energies;\n- correct worldline limits;\n- stable coupled behavior;\n- preserved benchmark recoveries.\n\nThis is where “works alone” becomes “works as part of H(s)H.”\n\n### 8. LaTeX/Publication Lab\n\nThe LaTeXer should receive a frozen evidence packet, not loose research conversation.\n\nIts job is representation:\n\n- exposition;\n- equation numbering;\n- dependency-respecting order;\n- tables and figures;\n- citations;\n- appendices;\n- reproducibility notes;\n- clear status language.\n\nIt should never decide that a candidate is established because it makes the paper read better.\n\n## What constitutes testing\n\nThe supervisor needs a precise test taxonomy.\n\n| Test | Question |\n|---|---|\n| Source-fidelity | Was the imported mathematics represented accurately and in scope? |\n| Object/type | Are the equations defined on the object SAT is actually using? |\n| Dimensional | Are units and geometric dimensions consistent? |\n| Internal consistency | Do definitions, constraints, and equations coexist? |\n| Well-posedness | Are solutions defined and appropriately regular? |\n| Thin-tube reduction | Does the worldtube recover the worldline model? |\n| Control/ablation | Does removing the proposed feature remove the target phenomenon? |\n| Benchmark | Does it reproduce the known result within declared tolerance? |\n| Holdout | Does it recover data not used to construct or calibrate it? |\n| Cross-sector | Does it explain an unrequested result elsewhere? |\n| Sensitivity | Is the result robust or dependent on delicate choices? |\n| Convergence | Does numerical refinement approach a stable answer? |\n| Conservation | Are claimed invariants preserved? |\n| Independent replication | Can a separate implementation recover the result? |\n| Integration | Does the module work with the rest of the backbone? |\n\nTesting should produce a structured result, not merely “the plot looked right.”\n\n## The promotion ladder\n\nI would preserve your scientific distinction explicitly:\n\n1. `EXTRACTED MACHINERY`  \n   Accurately recovered from a source.\n\n2. `ADAPTATION CANDIDATE`  \n   A plausible mapping into a specified H(s)H gap.\n\n3. `IMPLEMENTED CANDIDATE`  \n   Encoded and executable.\n\n4. `LOCALLY VALIDATED`  \n   Passes mathematical, numerical, and target-phenomenon tests.\n\n5. `HOLDOUT-VALIDATED`  \n   Recovers effects not used in its construction.\n\n6. `CROSS-SECTOR SUPPORTED`  \n   Produces useful unrequested consequences elsewhere.\n\n7. `INTEGRATION-VALIDATED`  \n   Coexists with the backbone and preserves established limits.\n\n8. `SAT MODEL CHOICE`  \n   Approved by you as part of the active construction.\n\nA numerical match alone cannot jump from stage 3 to stage 8.\n\n## The artifacts passed between labs\n\nEach laboratory should communicate through standardized packets rather than raw prose:\n\n- Worldline Win Record\n- Mathematical Machinery Record\n- SAT Translation Specification\n- Experiment Contract\n- Code and Run Record\n- Formalization Record\n- Audit Report\n- Integration Decision\n- Publication Evidence Packet\n\nThe supervisory skill would know how to create, inspect, reject, revise, and route these packets.\n\n## What the supervisor really is\n\nGiven this architecture, I would describe the future skill as:\n\n> A laboratory director and epistemic control system for recovering the validated SAT worldline baseline, governing the transition to H(s)H worldtubes, commissioning specialized LLM work, enforcing staged mathematical and computational testing, and controlling promotion into the integrated backbone.\n\nThat is more specific—and much more useful—than a generic evaluator or coach.\n\nBefore designing its actual instructions, I think our next discussion should define the worldline-to-worldtube module slots and the minimum contents of a “Worldline Win Record.” Those two structures determine what every laboratory will be trying to preserve or build."

            "Well... we *have* been treating the filament as having a thickness, bending, and so on. Cross-sectional geometry is perhaps a bit of a misnomer... if it's an ER bridge, then the cross-sectional geometry *accessible* (to the outside world) is just the thickness and interaction properties. Although we'd model it as an ER bridge, to the extent we know the cross-sectional geometry of that (we've been playing with a Kerr/ergosphere sort of model, and I see promise there; I think of the bounary as sort of event-horizon-like, and the intersection being identified as an ST closed string, perhaps with ergosphere/schwarzschild oscillations acting as vibration modes...although I think the consensus is that a naked ring singularity is more accurate... i'm not so sure). But we're leaning into the idea that this intersection is essentially a closed string. So... degrees of freedom...? I'm not sure that's changed that much. \n\nAs for Lean... I know what Lean is. Only CODATA conformal theory is valid as evidence (but internal consistency must be held above data-matching, so that only the right kind of matches count), and the best evidence can ever do is show that the model may be approaching an isometry with observation; no theory is ever proven, no ontology is ever accessible. I think Lean is probably most likely best for derivation checking. Although... if we can build a clean, tested version of the Whirligig and UI (and maybe a combined version)... then if we could encode *that* into Lean... or any similar geometric solver... then we might have something closer to the 'physical (read: model) adjucator'.\n\nAs for fossilization. Yes, that's a risk. But I do think there's room for lean or python in testing the speculations and hypotheses; I just cannot trust LLMs to do it much of the time (though we've had some success with 'python-locked' modularized setups). And I don't have the skills to check myself. But we'd want to have strictly siloed speculative and formal pipelines. \n\n\"Another NotebookLM can review whether code corresponds to supplied equations, but it cannot replace execution.\" C'mon, give me some credit. Code QC checks, as you say: Does it implement the math specified. But it can *only* check that. LLM Code QC (which I'm using strictly for pre-run code checking) *cannot* assess correct, reproducable output, nor mathematical behavior of the output. Ideally, checks for behaviors are done by the code itself. That is... we should be able to reliably predict when the code will work, and when the code will fail, based upon whether the input parameters are correct or dummy. And we should have an idea of what it should do when they're close but off in different ways. All of that is a *separate* later stage of evaluation, not Code QC. And NotebookLM cannot run code, so the running itself is either you (or an instance of you anyway), or me. \n\nOn the symbols front... we need a robust paradigm for symbol management and a set of rules for trackability. Eg, we'll be importing equations from far and wide, and we don't want to confuse the same symbol used in different contexts. But we also don't want to go willy nilly inventing new symbols. My suggestion is we have a simple list of super or sub pre or post script notations that indicate exactly where each notation comes from and use them consistently until we've solidified the math enough to decide what makes sense to do in terms of how we canonicaly notate different things. So, maybe it's just giving each source formalism a letter or number abbreviation or code and using it like... just making this up, but say QCD_n and BV_n or something, for n as used in QCD and BV Push-Forward respectively. Although existing scientific terms already do a *lot* of this, so maybe we choose something more distinctive that isn't in widespread use: \n\n🄶G for GR G, and 🄿G for PDE G, for example, or ƎᗡԀ_g ꓤ⅁_g. Or even ⰃG and ⰒG or G^ฆ and G^ผ ග_G and ප_G or Gກ and Gພ ...but whatever we do, do it consistently until it's time to stop compiling and testing formalism ideas, and time to forge our own formulation, then find an as-standard-as-possible system aimiing to minimize confusion. \n\nLaTeXer shoud be focused on derivation modules and explanations/background, and the other things you mentioned strictly supplementary to plaintext versions for a new, clean theory archive for real public use, and (later) papers for publication. \n\nAs for testing... yes, and scope, overall sanity check, and 'is there a better/simpler way' and 'can this be made to make more sense for a mainstream physics audience' and so on. \n\nAll of this could really easily become unweildy, so I think we should focus on designing for dependability *and* efficiency, with clear path-chain encoding. We may want to set up a repository for things like code running and report generation. \n\n\n\n"
